After a temporary acceleration to +4.7% in 2024,
growth in global real estate assets12 slowed again to
+3.5% in 2025. Apart from 2023, growth was weaker
only in the aftermath of the global financial crisis in
2012. Persistently high construction costs and elevated
mortgage rates continued to weigh on affordability,
transactions and new development. Overall, the value of
real estate assets in the countries covered by the report

reached EUR155.0trn (Figure 25)
Regional developments varied widely (Figure 26).
Eastern Europe recorded the strongest increase at
+10.6%, followed by Australia and New Zealand at
+8.5%. Both comfortably exceeded their long-term
growth rates despite the pressure from higher financing
costs. Japan also stood out: real-estate assets increased
by +5.0%, compared with a long-term average of
just +0.7%. Together with the recent acceleration in
household borrowing, this provides further evidence that

Japan may finally be leaving its long deflationary period
behind.
Western Europe recorded growth of +4.1%, slightly
above its long-term average of +3.5%. North America
meanwhile was the clear exception: growth slowed
sharply from +4.9% in 2024 to just +1.8% in 2025,
less than half its long-term rate of +3.9%. The global
slowdown was therefore driven primarily by the largest
housing market.

As with financial assets, a clearer picture emerges
when real-estate wealth is measured per capita and
adjusted for inflation (Figure 27). The long shadow of
Japan’s property crash remains visible: over the past two
decades, real-estate wealth per capita was essentially
flat in real terms, despite the recent recovery.
Real gains were positive in Australia and New Zealand,
North America and Western Europe, but generally
modest. Compared with financial assets, the gap was

particularly pronounced in North America, where real
financial assets per capita grew around 2pps faster
per year, and in Japan, where the gap was around
1.3pps. In Australia and New Zealand and Western
Europe, by contrast, the difference was only around
0.2pps annually. Property may provide stability, housing
services and diversification, but capital appreciation
alone has generally been a weaker engine of wealth
creation than participation in financial markets.

The country ranking of real estate assets nevertheless
shows how strongly housing can reshape the overall
picture. Switzerland had the highest real-estate assets
per capita at EUR350,450, followed by Australia
at EUR279,310 with the US taking the 7th spot at
EUR174,030. But what does the ranking look like when
net financial assets and real estate assets are presented
together? Figure 28 provides the answer. Switzerland
overtakes the US and claims first place, with combined

wealth of around EUR626,430 per capita. Australia
meanwhile is the biggest beneficiary, climbing seven
places from tenth in the adjusted net-financial-assets
ranking to third once property is included. Sweden
and Japan each fall six places, while Italy drops four.
Germany moves up one position to tenth. These shifts
expose two very different models of household wealth:
some countries build wealth predominantly through
financial markets, while others hold a much larger share

in bricks and mortar.

International: Catch-up loses momentum
For many years, global wealth development was
characterized by convergence, as emerging markets
gradually closed the gap with richer economies. That
process has not ended, but it has slowed dramatically.
The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in
advanced economies to those in emerging markets
fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015. This means that, on
average, the net financial assets of richer countries were

“only” 21 times higher than those of poorer countries in
2015. Over the following decade, however, it declined by
less than four points to 17 in 2025 (Figure 29).
Convergence reversed from 2017. After narrowing
rapidly for more than a decade, the wealth gap widened
again between 2017 and 2021. Convergence resumed
in 2022, but at a much slower pace: the ratio has fallen
from 18.5 to 17.2 in the past three years. The global
wealth gap is still closing, but has slowed to a crawl.

Declaring the end of globalization would be premature,
but its character has clearly changed. Geopolitical
tensions, weaker cross-border investment and the
reorganization of supply chains are eroding some of
the advantages that emerging markets derived from
ever-deeper global integration. At the same time, the
green transition and AI could create new opportunities
by increasing demand for critical resources, energy
infrastructure, data services and digitally delivered

work. Whether emerging economies can seize these
opportunities will depend on their access to technology,
capital and skills. For now, these transformations are
unfolding in a more fragmented world, creating a less
favorable environment for poorer countries to catch up.
Against this backdrop, the global wealth middle class
comprised around 866mn people in 2025.13 Although
its size changed little compared with the previous
year, its long-term expansion remains impressive:

membership has increased by 81% since 2005. More
importantly, its center of gravity has shifted. Emerging
markets accounted for 61.9% of its members in 2025,
up from 43.6% two decades earlier, with China alone
representing slightly more than half of the emerging-
market middle class.
Distribution:
New wealth, old divides

The transformation is even more striking among the
wealthy. The global high-wealth class grew by 37%

to around 584mn people between 2005 and 2025.
Emerging markets accounted for 32.1% of this group,
compared with less than 2% in 2005. This expansion
came entirely from emerging markets, while the number
of high-wealth individuals in advanced economies
declined slightly. China played the dominant role,
accounting for more than three-quarters of the
emerging-market total.
Emerging economies also generated most of the
growth in the global middle class. Taken together,

they accounted for more than 90% of the increase in
the middle and high-wealth classes over the past two
decades. The global wealth landscape has therefore
become far more diverse, even though the gap in
average wealth between advanced and emerging
markets remains wide.
The global low-wealth class grew by around 7% to
4.3bn people, while the population covered by the
analysis increased by almost 17%. Its share of the total
population consequently fell from 81.7% in 2005 to

74.8% in 2025. Emerging markets still accounted for
92.0% of this group, slightly below their 92.9% share two
decades earlier. However, the number of low-wealth
individuals in advanced economies increased by around
21% to 346mn. The global shift towards higher wealth
classes is therefore unmistakable, but it has by no means
benefited everyone.
The emergence of a larger and more geographically
diverse high-wealth class does not change the fact that

financial wealth remains extremely concentrated at the
global level. This becomes clear when the population of
the countries covered by the report is divided into deciles
according to net financial assets.
In 2025, the richest 10%, with average net financial
assets of approximately EUR312,590, owned 85.4% of
global net financial wealth. Their share has declined
from 91.5% in 2005, but remains far above the average
national top-decile share of 60.9% (Figure 30). At the

pace of improvement recorded over the past two
decades, it would take almost another eight decades
for global concentration to approach the level currently
observed within the countries.
13  The classification in wealth classes is based on worldwide average net financial assets per capita, which stood at EUR36,690 in 2025. The global
wealth middle class („middle wealth“, MW) includes all individuals with net financial assets of between 30% and 180% of the global average. This

means that for 2025, the rounded asset thresholds for the global wealth middle class are EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. The „low wealth“ (LW) catego -
ry, on the other hand, includes those individuals with net financial assets that are below a EUR11,000 threshold, while the term „high wealth“ (HW)
applies to those with net financial assets of more than EUR66,000 (for details on how the asset thresholds are set, see Appendix A).

### Page 38

Allianz Research
38
High global wealth concentration is also reflected in the
wide gap between median and average net financial
assets. In 2025, global median net financial wealth
stood at approximately EUR2,490 per capita, while the
average was almost 15 times as high at EUR36,690. The
gap, however, has narrowed substantially over time: in
2005, the average was almost 33 times the median. The
typical person has therefore gained ground relative to

the global average, but the average remains a poor
representation of ordinary wealth.
At first glance, the much faster growth of average net
wealth in most lower deciles appears encouraging
(Figure 31). However, these high growth rates largely
reflect extremely low starting points. Even large
percentage increases translate into modest absolute
gains, while the richest groups continue to accumulate
much larger sums. A more equal global distribution of

wealth therefore remains a distant prospect.

Bottom 50%
The gap between the richest decile and everyone else is

enormous. The second-richest decile owned just 8.7% of
global net financial assets, with an average of around
EUR34,060 per person. The richest tenth therefore held
nine times as much wealth per person as the group
immediately below it.
The bottom half of the population covered by the
report, comprising almost 3bn people, held virtually
nothing. Its combined share of global net financial
assets was approximately zero. The bottom decile had
negative net financial assets of around EUR2,680 per

person, as liabilities exceeded financial assets. Even
in the two deciles around the middle of the global
distribution, average net financial assets amounted to
only approximately EUR3,170 and EUR1,810 per person.
Global wealth has become more geographically diverse,
but it has hardly become broadly distributed.

### Page 39

29 September  2026
39
National: More wealth, same divide
Net financial wealth is distributed less unevenly within
countries than across the world. In 2025, the richest 10%
owned an average of 60.9% of national net financial
assets across the 57 countries examined, using an
unweighted average, compared with 85.4% at the global
level. The gap between average and median wealth
was also considerably smaller: the national average-
to-median ratio was 3.12, again measured as an

unweighted average.
When it comes to inequality, however, the national
picture is particularly disappointing. Despite being a
major political issue for years, there has been virtually
no progress towards more even wealth distribution.
In 2005, the richest 10% held an average of 60.4% of
national wealth, while the average-to-median ratio
stood at 3.11. Two decades later, both measures are
almost unchanged. Wealth has grown substantially, but
its distribution within individual countries has remained

remarkably resistant to change.
This overall stability conceals sharper shifts at the
country level. In 32 of the 57 countries examined, the
share of the richest 10% changed by less than 2pps
between 2005 and 2025 (Figure 32). Where larger
shifts occurred, however, the balance was clearly tilted
towards greater inequality, with deterioration almost
twice as likely as improvement. Wealth concentration
increased in 16 countries and declined in only nine.

Among those recording an improvement, Cambodia
saw the largest decline in the top-decile share, followed
by Portugal and Slovakia. Latvia, Hungary, Belgium,
Ireland, Malaysia and the Netherlands also registered
meaningful decreases. Different starting points were at
work. Portugal, Latvia and Ireland moved down from
relatively high levels of concentration, while Slovakia,
Hungary and the Netherlands improved from already
more balanced positions. In the Netherlands, the richest

10% saw their share fall by 2.4pps to 56.1%.
At the other end of the spectrum, China recorded by far
the largest increase in concentration. The share of the
richest 10% rose by 15.7pps between 2005 and 2025 to
68.0%, well above the average national share of 60.9%.
This accompanied the enormous economic and social
transformation of the past two decades, which created
not only broad-based wealth but also a substantial
private upper class. China illustrates the difficulty of

combining rapid wealth creation with an equally rapid
diffusion of its benefits. After all, it was the unbridled
growth of the „wild“ 2000s, when the private sector
was subject to few restrictions, that primarily caused
inequality to rise. In sharp contrast, in the last five years,
the concentration of wealth has remained unchanged.

The ratio of average to median wealth provides an
important cross-check: it shows whether rising average
wealth is also reflected in the position of the typical

household. Across the 57 countries examined, the
average ratio remained virtually unchanged, rising from
3.11 in 2005 to 3.12 in 2025. This confirms the broader
picture of persistent national wealth inequality.
The strongest improvements occurred in the
Netherlands, where the ratio fell from 4.04 to 2.48, and
Ireland, where it declined from 4.87 to 3.42. In both
countries, the narrowing gap indicates that the middle
of the distribution gained ground relative to average

wealth.
The largest deteriorations were recorded in Russia,
Sweden and China. Russia’s ratio rose from 2.99 to 4.77,
while Sweden’s increased from 3.77 to 5.25. In China, it
climbed from 2.03 to 3.33. Median Chinese net financial
wealth increased substantially, but average wealth grew
faster. China thus illustrates a central tension of rapid
wealth creation: prosperity can spread while inequality
rises at the same time.
More broadly, the limited movement in both distribution

measures across most countries suggests that wealth
inequality is deeply entrenched. Two decades of
economic growth, crises and political attention have
barely altered the underlying distribution.

China was not alone. The top-decile share increased by
10.5pps in Russia and by 6.8pps in India, where rising
wealth concentration is a “side effect” of rapid growth.
Concentration also rose in several advanced economies.

In the US, the richest 10% increased their share by 4.0pps
to 68.2%, one of the highest levels among advanced
markets and with no improvement in sight. Sweden and
Switzerland also recorded increases of more than 4pps.
France saw a smaller rise of 3.1pps, although its top-
decile share of 54.8% remained below Germany’s 59.5%.
Rising concentration is therefore not confined to emerging
economies, nor does rapid development inevitably produce
it.

Appendix A: Methodological comments
General assumptions
The Allianz Global Wealth Report analyses gross financial assets held by households, i.e. cash and bank deposits,
receivables from insurance companies and pension institutions, securities (shares, bonds and investment funds) and other
receivables, as well as household liabilities. Net financial assets are defined as gross financial assets minus liabilities.
Real estate is excluded from this measure and analyzed separately where data are available. The report is based

on data from 57 countries. This group of countries covers 91% of global GDP and 72% of the global population. In 43
countries, we had access to statistics from the macroeconomic financial accounts. In the other countries, we were able to
estimate the volume of total financial assets based on information from household surveys, bank statistics, statistics on
assets held in equities and bonds and technical reserves.
To eliminate exchange-rate distortions over time, financial assets and liabilities denominated in national currencies are

converted into euros using exchange rates at the end of 2025. Unless explicitly described as real, these values are not
adjusted for inflation. The closing date for data to be included in the report is 31 July.
Statistical distinctions
The process associated with the introduction of the European System of Accounts 2010 (ESA 2010) in September 2014
involved updating and harmonizing the guidelines governing the preparation of many macroeconomic statistics. The

new requirements also apply to the macroeconomic financial accounts. One change relates to private households: under
the ESVG 2010 regulations, the two sectors „Private households“ and „Non-profit institutions serving households“ are
now reported separately. This also has implications for the Allianz Global Wealth Report, which takes data from the
macroeconomic financial accounts as a basis where available. For many countries, however – particularly those outside

of the EU – there is no separate data available for these sectors in general. So in order to ensure global comparability,
this publication analyzes both sectors together under the heading „private households“.
Determination of wealth bands for global wealth classes
Lower wealth threshold: There is a close link between financial assets and the incomes of private households. According
to Davies et al. (2009) , private individuals with below-average income tend to have no assets at all, or only very few. It is

only when individuals move into middle and higher income groups that they start to accumulate substantial assets.
We have applied this link to our analysis. Countries in the upper-middle income bracket (based on the World Bank‘s
country classification system) therefore form the group in which the average household assets first reach a meaningful
level. This value marks the lower threshold for the global middle wealth class.
In terms of income, households with incomes that correspond to between 75% and 150% of average net income are

generally considered to constitute the middle class. According to Davies et al., households with income corresponding
to 75% of the average income have assets that correspond to 30% of the average assets. As far as the upper threshold
is concerned, 150% of average income corresponds to 180% of average assets. Consequently, we have set the threshold
values for the wealth middle class at 30% and 180% of average net financial assets per capital. In 2025, average net

financial assets per capita in the countries covered stood at approximately EUR36,690. Applying these percentages gives
the rounded thresholds used in the workbook: EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. Individuals with net financial assets between
these thresholds belong to the global middle-wealth class. Those below the lower threshold belong to the low-wealth
class, while those above the upper threshold belong to the high-wealth class.

14  Davies, James B. et al. (2009), The level and distribution of global household wealth, NBER working paper 15508.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN DWELLING
APPROVALS BULLETIN
Unit approvals drive the headline lower
30 September 2026
Key points
• Total dwelling approvals fell 6.1%mth in
August, with the annual pace easing to
10.3%yr. The headline decline was driven
by a sharp fall in private unit approvals.

• Private detached dwelling approvals
rose 3.7%mth, reaching their highest
level since August 2021. In contrast,
private unit approvals fell 21.2%mth.
• Queensland recorded the weakest
result, while NSW also posted
a sizeable decline. By contrast,
WA, Victoria and South Australia
recorded gains over the month.
• The value of residential building
approvals fell 0.6%mth. Volatility
in the data centre dominated ‘other
commercial buildings’ segment
contributed to the total value of

building approvals falling 21.3%mth.
• While detached dwelling approvals
continue to show resilience, recent
RBA cash rate increases continue
to present risks to the outlook.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Total dwelling approvals fell 6.1%mth in August, with the
annual pace easing slightly to 10.3%yr. The result was broadly
in line with Westpac’s expectation for a 5.0%mth decline,
although notably weaker than the market expectation for a
1.0%mth fall. July’s decline in private detached dwellings

proved short lived, with approvals rebounding in August.
Instead, weakness in the headline result was driven by a
sizeable decline in private unit approvals.
Note that most figures in the charts and table are presented on
a rolling-three month basis to smooth out monthly volatility.
Private detached dwelling approvals rose 3.7%mth in August,
following a large 2.5ppt upward revision to July’s 1.9%mth
decline. The increase was the strongest in more than a year,

with private detached dwelling approvals reaching their highest
level since August 2021. With the annual pace strengthening
to 18.4%yr, detached dwelling approvals continue to appear
resilient despite recent interest rate increases. Some of this
resilience may partly reflect the implementation of changes to
SMSF property borrowing arrangements on 10 August.
Private unit approvals drove the decline in the headline result,
falling 21.2%mth in August. Following a 1.2ppt upward

revision to July’s 0.8%mth increase, the annual pace returned to
contractionary territory at –2.1%yr. Weakness was broad-based
across segments, with both high-rise and low-mid-rise
approvals declining in the month.
Queensland recorded the weakest result, with private approvals
falling 22.6%mth, driven primarily by weakness in unit approvals,
while private approvals in NSW also declined, falling 17.1%mth.
By contrast, WA (+0.2%mth), Victoria (+8.4%mth) and South

Australia (+32.0%mth) all recorded gains over the month.
In value terms, residential building approvals fell 0.6%mth,
driven by a 0.5%mth decline in new residential approvals
and a 2.1%mth fall in renovation activity. Non-residential
approvals declined sharply, down 44.8%mth. Volatility
continues to be driven by the data centre-dominated ‘other
commercial’ segment, which fell 91.1%mth on a non-seasonally
adjusted basis. Excluding this segment, in non-seasonally

adjusted terms, non-residential approvals fell a more subdued
13.9%mth (from -37.9%mth). The total value of building
approvals declined 21.3%mth in August.
August’s result suggests dwelling approvals continue to
moderate following the strength recorded earlier in the year.
However, the underlying picture remains more resilient, with
the headline decline driven by volatility in unit approvals
while detached dwellings strengthened. NSW and Victoria,

traditionally among the more interest rate sensitive housing
markets, recorded the weakest detached dwelling outcomes,
potentially signalling broader rate effects ahead. For a
comprehensive assessment of housing market conditions, see
our latest Housing Pulse (see here).
Detached approvals point to some resilience

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -49.02 điểm tương ứng -2.66%, với thanh khoản cải 
thiện. Dệt may, Y tế và Bất động sản là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt nhất trong 
tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -4,944 tỷ đồng, 
tập trung ở các mã VPB, TCB và VHM; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều mua ròng ở mức 1995 
tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại bán ròng -5,280 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần qua và đang 
duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế 3,630 hợp đồng. Tự doanh mua ròng 208 hợp đồng nhưng đang 
duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -6,428 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam tiếp tục điều chỉnh tuần thứ hai liên tiếp sau nâng hạng, khi VN-Index lùi 
về đóng cửa 1.737 điểm — giảm khoảng 2% so với tuần trước và đang kiểm định lại vùng 
đáy cũ của tháng 8, quanh 1.720. Áp lực bán duy trì khi chỉ số giảm nhiều phiên liên tiếp, 
cho thấy hiệu ứng "mua kỳ vọng, bán thực tế" sau nâng hạng vẫn chưa kết thúc, trong bối 
cảnh dòng tiền thận trọng và thanh khoản ở mức thấp. Khối ngoại tiếp tục là lực cản 
chính khi bán ròng mạnh cả 5 phiên trong tuần, riêng phiên 2/10 bán ròng tới 3.361 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở PNJ và HDB. Áp lực bên ngoài vẫn hiện diện khi Fed duy trì lập trường 
diều hâu và giá dầu ở mức cao; trong một, hai tuần tới, mùa báo cáo KQKD sẽ bắt đầu có 
tác động, kỳ vọng theo chiều hướng tích cực, qua đó giúp nâng đỡ thị trường chung.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX hình thành cây nến giảm điểm tiêu cực trên khung tuần, xuyên thủng vùng hỗ trợ 
gần quanh 1750, cho thấy áp lực bán đã có phần quyết liệt hơn sau 2 tuần giằng co trước 
đó. Trên khung ngày, sau các phiên lình xình vào đầu tuần, chỉ số cũng dần chuyển sang 
diễn biến giảm điểm rõ nét hơn về cuối tuần, đồng thời cắt sâu xuống dưới SMA50, thể 
hiện áp lực phân phối đang có phần lấn át mặc dù chưa xảy ra hiện tượng bán tháo khi 
khối lượng khớp lệnh được giữ ở mức thấp. Diễn biến tính từ vùng đỉnh 1870 vào đầu tháng 
9 đang không mấy khả quan, tuy nhiên xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn, tính từ đáy 1640 vào cuối 
tháng 7, hiện vẫn đang được bảo lưu và VNINDEX được kỳ vọng sẽ có nhịp hồi phục trở lại 
khi về đến vùng hỗ trợ kế tiếp tại quanh 1710.

Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.

the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.

y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,

beyon

Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.

MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.
Exhibit 1: Scott Bessent's Three-Front War.
The Front The Problem Bessent's Objective The Weapon
The Yen Weak yen/currency instability Stronger yen Foreign Exchange intervention, policy coordination, pressure on Japan
The Yield Rising 10-year Treasury yield Lower/contained yields Debt-management strategy, buybacks, issuance policy

Iran Tehran's access to global finance Maximum financial pressure Sanctions, asset restrictions, dollar-system leverage
Source: Chief Investment Office.
For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

2 BloombergNEF , AI Buildout Drives 76% Power Bill Jump on Largest US Grid, May 14, 2026.

Investment Implications
Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

Economy 
Energy bills: Short-term relief, longer-term challenges 
16 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Energy bill inflation was around 4% year-over-year (YoY) in August 2026. However, Bank of America internal deposit data shows 
average utility bill payments rose faster, increasing 5% YoY from June through August. This was likely due, in part, to higher 
cooling demand during a warm summer. 
• But bill pressures remain highly uneven across the country. Average utility payments have risen by more than 10% YoY in cities 
such as Detroit, Baltimore and Washington DC, while consumers in Orlando, San Jose and Tampa have seen declines. Some of 
these regional differences appear to reflect policy and infrastructure factors as much as energy usage itself. 
• The long-term outlook still points to upward pressure on electricity bills as demand from data center construction, electrification 
and broader economic activity drive the need for additional generation and grid investment. However, a forecasted strong El 
Niño event this winter could provide some near-term relief, if warmer-than-normal temperatures reduce household heating 
demand, partially offsetting structural cost pressures.

Energy bill inflation has moderated  
Energy is an essential household expense. According to Bureau of Labor Statistics data, energy bills accounted for roughly 3% of 
total household spending as of 2024. US utility prices have been far less affected by higher global oil prices stemming from the 
conflict in the Middle East than gasoline prices, largely because the US is a net exporter of natural gas. Nonetheless, the cost of 
energy has been rising for a prolonged period: since January 2024 the price of electricity has risen 10% and the price of piped 
gas is up 18%.  
The better news is that utility price inflation has moderated relative to the second half of 2025 and early 2026. As of August, 
electricity and piped gas prices were up around 4% year-over-year (YoY). But Bank of America internal deposit data shows that 
average utility payments for electricity and gas were running ahead of inflation in these categories, up 5.3% YoY. One reason 
could be that household demand for cooling was strong this summer, due to relatively warm weather (Exhibit 1).  
Exhibit 1: Utility bill payments rose around 5.3% YoY in August

Energy costs vary widely by region 
While energy bills account for a relatively modest share of household spending nationally, experiences vary across the country. 
Looking at large cities (by Core Based Statistical Areas), our data shows that utility bills rose by more than 10% YoY in Detroit, 
Baltimore and Washington DC from June through August 2026. At the other end of the spectrum, bills fell YoY in Orlando, San 
Joe and Tampa (Exhibit 2).  
Customers’ bills are being pressured by reshoring, reindustrialization, the general pass-through costs in the economy, and a 
significant need for infrastructure replacement in many markets. Regulatory changes and one-off charges have also influenced 
costs in several areas. In Florida, for example, the removal of hurricane recovery surcharges reduced utility bills in 2026, while in 
the Northeast, investments in grid infrastructure and capacity have contributed to higher costs.  
There also remains some disparities in where average bills sit relative to the overall US (Exhibit 3). In Washington DC, Boston, 
Phoenix and Dallas, the average utility bill payment in June through August 2026 was well above the US average. Conversely, 
Chicago, Nashville and Las Vegas paid some of the lowest energy bills relative to the US.

Rising demand for electricity will continue to pressure bills  
Looking longer-term, rising energy demand (particularly for electricity), driven by factors such as data center expansion and 
residential electrification, is likely to keep upward pressure on consumers’ energy bills for a number of years (read Is the AI 
revolution raising consumers' utility bills? and Can the AI buildout keep building? for more).  
Exhibit 4 shows forecasts from the US Energy Information Administration’s August 2026 Short-term Energy Outlook, estimating 
a 4% YoY rise in commercial and industrial electricity consumption. Accommodating this growth will likely require ongoing 
investment in grid capacity and power generation, and some of these costs may be passed on to consumers through higher 
energy bills.  
Exhibit 4: The demand for electricity is rising particularly from commercial and industrial sectors 
US electrical demand (in billion kilowatt hours) 2020-2027E (estimates/forecast from 2026)

Source: US Energy Information Administration 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Short-term, the weather could prove important  
El Niño is the warming phase of ENSO (El Niño–Southern Oscillation), a global climate phenomenon that emerges from variation 
in winds and sea surface temperatures over the Pacific Ocean. El Niño events can have widespread and profound global impacts, 
causing extreme weather such as storms, floods and higher temperatures. The strongest recorded El Niño took place in 1877 to 
1878 and led to a global famine that killed more than 50 million people.  
The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) believes there is a high probability that a very strong El Niño 
event will occur this year (Exhibit 5). One way to put this in historical context is through the NOAA’s Relative Oceanic Niño Index 
(RONI). A positive RONI indicates the central/eastern tropical Pacific is unusually warm relative to the rest of the tropics. A 
higher RONI indicates a more severe El Niño, though NOAA points out that uncertainty on impacts also tends to rise with RONI.  
Exhibit 5: The NOAA attaches a very high probability to a severe El Niño event over coming months

For the US, significant El Niño events tend to bring higher-than-usual temperatures and more rainfall (Exhibit 7). However, no El 
Niño is exactly the same. For example, in 1997-98 California was flooded in but in 2015-16 it was dry, while the nation overall 
was wet. Both time periods were among the highest levels of RONI in the past 70 years.

What does this mean for consumers and their energy bills? The most predictable impact from a severe El Niño appears to be 
unseasonably warm temperatures, implying reduced heating needs during the fall and winter. Consumers could therefore receive 
some relief in their utility bills and potentially have additional income to spend on discretionary goods and services over the 
coming months.  
There is a large caveat, however. If El Niño manifests in stormy and rainy conditions – likely impacting the southern parts of the 
US – then some households may end up having to spend on repairs and maintenance following weather damage (read more on 
consumer spending in the aftermath of natural disasters in October’s What's the impact of natural disasters on local 
economies?). This might boost consumer discretionary spending, but not in a way that implies improved well-being. And longer 
term, the broader impact of both climate change and energy demands from the AI revolution are likely to keep upward pressure 
on bills.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Islamic finance is growing at a rapid pace, driven 
primarily by increasing demand for investors who 
seek Shariah -compliant investments. Sukuk, often 
referred to as Islamic bonds, surpassed USD 1trn in 
2025, making them one of the fastest -growing 
segments o f Islamic finance. The asset class’s 
unique value proposition is driving its popularity, 
especially in an increasingly volatile global  
economic landscape. 
In this publication, we will explore the evolving role of Sukuk in wealth management, 
examining market fundamentals, risk frameworks and emerging opportunities within 
this market.

### Page 3

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  3

Sukuk, commonly referred to as Islamic bonds, are Shariah-compliant investment 
certificates representing ownership interests in underlying assets, usufructs (the 
right to use an asset and receive its benefits, without owning it) or ventures. In 
return, Sukuk holders receive periodic distributions, typically in the form of rental 
payments (Ijarah), profit-sharing (Mudarabah) or asset-generated cash flows, and 
upon maturity the principal amount is repaid. Sukuk differ from conventional 
bonds in several ways: 
Fig. 1  
Sukuk vs. conventional bonds

Aspect  Conventional bonds Sukuk

Core principle  Lending (time-value of money) Asset ownership 
Underlying asset  Any asset, business or project Must be Sharia-compliant 
Returns  Guaranteed (fixed or floating interest) Not legally guaranteed (depends on 
underlying asset’s performance) 
Risk   Issuer bears all performance risk Shared risk between parties 
Transparency  Moderate (depends on disclosure) High (asset identification required)

Source: Standard Chartered

The role of Sukuk in the investing landscape is evolving amid 
global uncertainty and rising geopolitical tensions. Investors 
are increasingly seeking more stable and asset ‑backed 
alternatives. An investor can consider investing in Sukuk for 
the following reasons: 
Resilient 
Sukuk provide asset -backed collateral that conventional 
bonds often lack, offering a cushion during volatile market 
conditions. Historically, Islamic investments, including Sukuk’, 
have been countercyclical, i.e. they tend to expand or remain 
stable when c onventional markets contract. Across major 
market stress from the 2018 US-China Trade War through the 
2026 Middle East Conflict, Sukuk bonds consistently 
outperformed Global Bonds — delivering smaller drawdowns 
in risk-off episodes such as the 2018 US-China trade war and 
2022 higher for longer inflation, and greater resilience in 
geopolitical oil shocks, underscoring the asset class's 
structural defensive characteristics.

Strong fundamentals
The Sukuk market experienced an unprecedented 25% growth in issuance in 2025, with issuance nearly USD 300bn. According
to some  reports,  the  Sukuk  market  could  reach  USD  3trn  by  2030  driven  by  increased  demand  for  Shariah-compliant
investments, sovereign funds’ diversification needs, development of regulatory frameworks for Sukuk and growth in Middle East

capital markets.  Nearly  80%  of  Fitch ‑rated Sukuk  carry  investment ‑grade ratings  and  have  little  or  no  history  of  default,
underscoring the sector’s strong fundamentals. These factors should help to instil confidence among investors, who can look at
Sukuk as central building blocks of their portfolio.

Convergence with sustainable investing trends
Sukuk and sustainable bonds share more common ground than their different origins suggest, making sustainable Sukuk a
natural bridge between Islamic finance and sustainable investing. This also helps explain Sukuk’s growing appeal beyond
traditional Muslim investors, as evidenced by Sustainable Sukuk issuance rising from USD 464mn in 2017 to USD 9bn in 2024,

representing a CAGR of 52.7%. The overlap starts with what Sukuk exclude: Shariah principles prohibit interest, excessive
uncertainty and speculation, and screen out sectors financing harmful industries such as tobacco, gambling, weapons, adult
entertainment and alcohol – a negative screen that closely aligns with the exclusionary (negative screening) method, commonly
used in sustainable investing. Tradi tional Sukuk bonds require proceeds to be linked to identifiable real assets or projects,

creating a ring-fenced structure like use-of-proceeds sustainable bonds. Combined with Shariah’s broader objectives of social
welfare, resource stewardship and equita ble risk-sharing, this creates strong alignment with the United Nations Sustainable
Development Goals.
Green Sukuk is an established example, where proceeds are earmarked for environmental projects such as renewable energy,
clean transport, energy-efficient buildings or sustainable water and wastewater management. Issuers must meet both Shariah

approval from a recognised scholarly board and alignment with International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond
Principles.
Traditional Sukuk already comply with Shariah laws such as avoiding Riba (interest -based returns arising from lending
transactions), Gharar (investments with ambiguity) and Maysir (speculation). Sustainable Sukuk build on this foundation by
adding environmental and social objectives. The table below highlights how sustainable Sukuk differ from traditional Sukuk.

Fig. 4
Traditional Sukuk vs. Sustainable bonds vs Sustainable Sukuk

Digital integration
The integration  of  blockchain  technology  and  digital  platforms  is  revolutionising  Sukuk  issuance  and  trading,  dramatically
reducing transaction costs and expanding accessibility to  a broader  investor base.  For instance, SAKKEX (an  independent
blockchain-based Sukuk marketplace) streamlines drafting, issuance, and near ‑instant settlement. Its Sukuk Wallet App also
allows investors to trade on the go. Several such digital platforms now allow tokenised Sukuk that enable fractional ownership,

automated Shariah  compliance  verification  and  real-time  settlement,  thereby  addressing  challenges  that  have  persisted  in
Islamic finance markets. Digital tools also simplify cross border Sukuk transactions by enabling end to end digital origination and
trading, with smart contracts automating profit sharing to cut operational costs and enhance transparency.

### Page 6

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026 6
Sukuk structures define the nature of ownership, risk -sharing and return
generation. Several types of Sukuk structures exist and are defined by the
underlying Shariah contract. We discuss some prominent ones below and see
how they align with various phases of an investor’s wealth journey:
Sukuk al-Ijarah is a Shariah-compliant lease-based bond where the investor gains partial ownership in a physical

asset such as a building, airport or equipment. The asset is then leased back to the issuer who pays a nominal
(pre-agreed) rent to the investor. An investor seeking stable, predictable income streams with lower volatility can
consider investing in these Sukuk. The original principal amount is returned at the end of the lease period.
Ijarah Sukuk are ideal for the wealth preservation phase of an investor’s wealth journey. For instance, investors
nearing retirement typically need to restructure their portfolios by shifting from growth to preservation while

generating steady income to cover living expenses. Ijarah Sukuk deliver on both fronts: protecting capital through
asset-backed structures while providing predictable rental income that supports household needs without
depleting principal.
Sukuk al-Murabahah are cost-plus financing structures where the issuer buys an asset and immediately resells
it to the investors at a marked-up price, with payment deferred over a pre-determined time. Consider this: Let us

assume, a company needs USD 100m. A special purpose vehicle (SPV), established by the company, mobilises
funds from investors and buys commodities worth USD 100m and immediately resells them to the company for
USD 112m payable over time. The USD 12m difference is the agreed profit margin. Murabahah Sukuk ar e best
suited for investors seeking income over a short to medium term, typically, 1-5 years. Investments in this type of
Sukuk return the principal gradually throughout the investment period rather than only at maturity.

Investments in Murabahah Sukuk are best suited for wealth protection and income generation. Unlike Ijarah which
return principal as a lump sum at maturity, Murabahah amortise both profit and principal in each instalment. This
makes them ideal for retirees who need to systematically draw down capital to fund living expenses without
depleting their portfolio too quickly.
Sukuk al-Mudarabah is a profit-sharing partnership arrangement where investors provide 100% of the capital

and operate as silent partners. The issuer manages the business or project as the active partner. Under a
Mudarabah arrangement, financial losses arising from normal bu siness risks are borne by the investors in
proportion to their capital contribution, while the Mudarib loses its effort and entitlement to profit. However, where
losses result from negligence, misconduct, breach of agreed terms, or misconduct by the Mudari b, the Mudarib
may be held liable. Think of it as venture capital meets bonds; you fund the business, professionals run it and

you share the profits or bear the losses resulting from the business activities. Mudarabah Sukuk are often
regarded as one of the  structures most closely aligned with the Shariah principle of profit and loss sharing.
However, they bear higher risk and lower predictability compared to other structures.
Investors with a growth mindset and open to high -risk tolerance can consider investing in these Sukuk . They
best align with accumulation and growth phase of the wealth journey, typically, when investors have long time

horizons to absorb volatility and can afford capital risk for higher returns. However, to balance risk, it is important
for investors to diversify across various types of Sukuk. Even within Mudarabah, they can consider investing
in multiple projects, sectors and geographies.
1 Sukuk structures and
wealth mandates

### Page 7

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  7

A joint venture partnership structure where both investors and the issuer contribute capital is Sukuk al -
Musharakah. In this structure, both parties (issuer and investors) jointly provide capital, participate in managing 
the venture, and share profits or losses based on agreed upon ratios. This structure appeals to investors focused 
on growth, interested in active participation, and prepared to take on greater risk in pursuit of higher returns. 
Musharakah Sukuk are best suited for the growth and accumulation phase where investors are building wealth 
aggressively, comfortable with high risk and able to lock up capital for the long term (typically 5+ years).

Sukuk al-Wakalah is an agency arrangement where investors appoint a representative from the issuing company 
as their Wakil (agent) to invest their funds in specific Shariah-compliant assets or projects in exchange for a fee. 
This arrangement is similar to a mutual fund or ETF, where the agent manages a diversified pool of Shariah 
compliant assets on behalf of the investors. Investors benefit from professional fund management, diversification, 
transparency and flexibility. Depending on issuer preferences, Wakalah Sukuk can b e structured with lower 
investment thresholds, making them accessible to a wide range of investors. 
Wakalah Sukuk suitability depends heavily on the underlying asset allocation and risk profile. Conservative 
Wakalah structures investing in stable, income -generating assets suit wealth preservation. Moderate -risk 
Wakalah can complement accumulation portfolios as a diversification tool, though not as a primary growth driver 
as their structure and risk mandate limit upside potential.

Sukuk al-Istisna is a project financing structure based on a manufacturing or construction contract. Investors 
provide upfront capital to finance the construction of specific assets. Upon completion, the asset is sold to an end 
buyer (often the original issuer or a third party) who makes deferred payments including the cost plus a mark-up, 
which are then distributed to investors. Istisna suit seasoned investors with construction finance expertise and 
those who can absorb development risk and commit capital for the long term. Owing to its complexity and higher 
risk profile, this type of Sukuk typically requires large initial investments, making it less accessible to retail 
investors.  
In the wealth cycle, Istisna are best aligned with the accumulation and growth phase, when long time horizons 
can absorb construction delays and development risks. The long-term commitment and potential for cost overruns 
or project failure make it unsuitable for later wealth stages such as retirement.

Sukuk al -Salam is an Islamic bond backed by a forward purchase contract where investors pay upfront for 
commodities to be delivered later. At its core, they are similar to forward contracts. However, unlike a forward 
contract, Salam contracts are fully prepaid, standardi sed and designed with clearly defined delivery terms to 
reduce uncertainty (gharar). Under this arrangement, an investor profits from the difference between the lower 
Salam purchase price (paid today) and the higher resale price (received at delivery).  
Salam Sukuk carry high delivery and price risk and aligns well with investors in the growth and accumulation 
phase. Salam requires strong commodity‑market expertise, deep understanding of production and delivery risks, 
and the ability to assess the issuer’s operational capacity to deliver the specified goods on time.

Owing to their ethical framework and asset -backed nature, Sukuk are often

immune to risks. Unique challenges such as asset quality deterioration and
structure-specific exposures require rigorous risk management.

Due diligence in Sukuk extends beyond traditional credit
analysis and an investor should also consider the following:
• Shariah compliance verification can be done by evaluating

the Sukuk structure and confirming that its mandates and
underlying assets fully align with Islamic principles.
• Evaluating asset quality by verifying tangibility, cash -
generating capability and legal ownership can help
investor reduce risk.
• Assessing creditworthiness and market depth will help in
identifying financially strong Sukuk issuers and ensuring
adequate liquidity.
• Cross-border Sukuk investments remain viable,
contingent on careful scrutiny of legal documentation

across multiple jurisdictions to ensure enforceability and
investor protection.
Robust due diligence and proactive risk mitigation strategies
are essential to protect capital and stay aligned with Islamic
financial principles.
1 Risk management and due diligence

### Page 10

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  10

Staying up to date on the latest developments in 
the Sukuk market is important as this space is 
constantly evolving and rapidly expanding. 
Moreover, Islamic finance is not static; Shariah 
interpretations related to Sukuk structures are 
periodically redefined. Staying informed protects 
investors from investing in non -compliant 
options. We look at some of the factors that are 
guiding the future of this landscape:

1 Emerging trends

Green Sukuk issuance is projected to grow materially through 
2030, reflecting the natural convergence of Islamic finance and 
sustainability principles. Key sectors include renewable 
energy, green buildings, low -carbon transportation, water and 
waste management and sustainable agriculture.

Tech advancements  are revolutionising Sukuk origination, 
trading and accessibility. Digitisation offers numerous benefits 
to the Islamic finance landscape: streamlined issuance and 
trading processes, enhanced accessibility through 
tokenisation, immutable audit trails via bl ockchain technology 
and improved secondary market liquidity.

A major regulatory shift is likely to create standardised rules 
across Islamic finance markets globally. The pivotal 
development is AAOIFI's Standard 62, launching in 2026, 
which mandates transparent, Shariah-compliant asset-backing 
and genuine asset ownership. This can fundament ally 
strengthen the Islamic authenticity of Sukuk structures.

The Sukuk market is rapidly expanding beyond its traditional 
Islamic finance strongholds. Non-traditional territories  in 
Africa (e.g., Algeria, Benin and Nigeria), Europe (e.g., 
Luxemburg, UK) and Asia (e.g., Turkey, Pakistan) are 
emerging as Islamic capital market gateways. This expansion 
underscores the evolution of Sukuk from a niche Islamic 
product to a globally reco gnised asset class attracting diverse 
sovereigns and investors across continents.

### Page 11

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  11

Conclusion

Ultimately, Sukuk demonstrate that Shariah compliance and sound investment 
strategy are not competing priorities, but complementary ones. By ensuring that 
portfolios are grounded in real economic activity by offering stability through 
tangible asset -backing and transparent structures that channel capital towards 
productive use. Their foundation in ethical, responsible financing appeals to 
investors seeking both resilience and purpose. As global markets increasingly 
prioritise integrity and long-term sustainability, Sukuk stand out as a compelling tool 
for Shariah -compliant investors who want meaningful diversification without 
compromising on their religious and ethical principles.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
NOVEMBER CPI
PREVIEW AND ANALYSIS
Complete Montly CPI will take some time to understand
19 December 2025
Key points
• The October monthly CPI was 
flat in the month, broadly as we 
expected but stronger than market 
expectations. For more information see 
“Electrical noise is far from static”.
• The impact on our inflation profile 
was marginal. The December quarter 
CPI remains 0.6%qtr/3.6%yr, while 
the Trimmed Mean edged up from 
0.7%qtr/3.1%yr to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr.
• Our November monthly CPI estimate 
is 0.4% for the month, leaving the 
annual pace unchanged at 3.8%yr.
• The introduction of the complete 
monthly CPI is a significant enhancement 
to our analysis of price formation in 
the Australian economy. However, 
with many series having only a short 
history, it will take time to fully 
understand some of the finer details.
• For now, we will continue to place 
greater emphasis on the quarterly 
measures of core inflation.
